Used to provide a release\\n36: // valve and ensure some level of quality-of-service under severe per-key\\n37: // contention. If set to a non-zero value and an existing lock wait-queue is\\n38: // already equal to or exceeding this length, the request will be rejected\\n39: // eagerly instead of entering the queue and waiting.\\n40: //\\n41: // This is a fairly blunt mechanism to place an upper bound on resource\\n42: // utilization per lock wait-queue and ensure some reasonable level of\\n43: // quality-of-service for transactions that enter a lock wait-queue. More\\n44: // sophisticated queueing alternatives exist that account for queueing time and\\n45: // detect sustained queue growth before rejecting:\\n46: // - https://queue.acm.org/detail.cfm?id=2209336\\n47: // - https://queue.acm.org/detail.cfm?id=2839461\\n48: //\\n49: // We could explore these algorithms if this setting is too coarse grained and\\n50: // not serving its purpose well enough.\\n51: //\\n52: // Alternatively, we could implement the lock_timeout session variable that\\n53: // exists in Postgres (#67513) and use that to ensure quality-of-service for\\n54: // requests that wait for locks. The\\n44: \\t// request will likely want to eventually push the conflicting transaction.\\n45: \\twaitFor\\n46: \\n47: \\t// waitElsewhere is used when the lockTable is under memory pressure and is\\n48: \\t// clearing its internal queue state. Like the waitFor* states, it informs\\n49: \\t// the request who it is waiting for so that deadlock detection works.\\n50: \\t// However, sequencing information inside the lockTable is mostly discarded.\\n51: \\twaitElsewhere\\n52: \\n53: \\t// waitSelf indicates that a different request from the same transaction has\\n54: \\t// claimed (but not acquired) the lock already. This request should sit tight\\n55: \\t// and wait for a new notification without pushing anyone.\\n56: \\t//\\n57: \\t// It must be the case that there are no lock holders for the associated key\\n58: \\t// if a request is actively waiting in the waitSelf state. That's because if\\n59: \\t// the key is locked by another transaction, the request must be in the\\n60: \\t// waitFor state. A request does not need to actively wait if the only lock\\n61: \\t// that's held on the key belongs to its own transaction, thus making the\\n62: \\t// waitSelf state impossible for held locks.\\n63: \\t//\\n64: \\t// NB: The main motivation of the waitSelf state is to avoid certain\\n65: \\t// dependency cycles by potentially re-ordering the lock's wait queue.\\n66: \\t// Typically, the waitSelf state arises when there are >= 1 locking requests\\n67: \\t// from other transactions between two requests from the same transaction\\n68: \\t// waiting in a lock's wait queue. So something like:\\n69: \\t//\\n70: \\t// key a: unlocked\\n71: \\t// wait-queue: [(r1,txn1,inactive), (r2,txn2,active), ... (r3,txn1,active)]\\n72: \\t//\\n73: \\t// There is a dependency cycle between the requests from txn1 and txn2 here.\\n74: \\t// Instead of forcing one of the two transaction's to abort, we're able to\\n75: \\t// break the dependency cycle by having r3 wait and sit tight in the waitSelf\\n76: \\t// state[1]. Now, if r1 acquires the lock, r3 will be re-ordered[2] to proceed\\n77: \\t// before r2 (thus breaking the dependency cycle); if it doesn't, r2 will no\\n78: \\t// longer depend on txn1 and can thus proceed (i.e. no more dependency cycle).\\n79: \\t//\\n80: \\t// [1] A transaction can't push itself, but if it finds the request at the\\n81: \\t// head of the queue everyone is pushing belongs to its own transaction, it\\n82: \\t// knows there is a local dependency cycle.\\n83: \\t//\\n84: \\t// [2] Note that re-ordering happens only when r1 acquires the lock, so the\\n85: \\t// waitSelf state is a mechanism by which r3 is able to sit tight without\\n86: \\t// pushing anyone.\\n87: \\twaitSelf\\n88: \\n89: \\t// waitQueueMaxLengthExceeded indicates that the request attempted to enter a\\n90: \\t// lock wait-queue as a locking request and found that the queue's length was\\n91: \\t// already equal to or exceeding the request's configured maximum. As a\\n92: \\t// result, the request was rejected.\\n93: \\twaitQueueMaxLengthExceeded\\n94: \\n95: \\t// doneWaiting indicates that the request is done waiting on this pass\\n96: \\t// through the lockTable and should make another call to ScanAndEnqueue.\\n97: \\tdoneWaiting\\n98: )\\n99: \\n100: // The current waiting state of the request.\\n101: //\\n102: // See the detailed comment about \\\"Waiting logic\\\" on lockTableGuardImpl.\\n103: type waitingState struct {\\n104: \\tkind waitKind\\n105: \\n106: \\t// Fields below are only populated for waitFor*, waitElsewhere and\\n107: \\t// waitQueueMaxLengthExceeded kinds.\\n108: \\n109: \\t// Represents who the request is waiting for. The conflicting\\n110: \\t// transaction may be a lock holder of a conflicting lock or a\\n111: \\t// conflicting request being sequenced through the same lockTable.\\n112: \\ttxn                   *enginepb.TxnMeta // always non-nil\\n113: \\tkey                   roachpb.Key       // the key of the conflict\\n114: \\theld                  bool              // is the conflict a held lock?\\n115: \\tqueuedLockingRequests int               // how many locking requests are waiting?\\n116: \\tqueuedReaders         int               // how many readers are waiting?\\n117: \\n118: \\t// Represents the lock strength of the action that the request was trying to\\n119: \\t// perform when it hit the conflict. E.g. was it trying to perform a (possibly\\n120: \\t// locking) read or write an Intent?\\n121: \\tguardStrength lock.Strength\\n122: }\\n123: \\n124: // String implements the fmt.Stringer interface.\\n125: func (s waitingState) String() string {\\n126: \\treturn redact.StringWithoutMarkers(s)\\n127: }\\n128: \\n129: // SafeFormat implements the redact.SafeFormatter interface.\\n130: func (s waitingState) SafeFormat(w redact.SafePrinter, _ rune) {\\n131: \\tswitch s.kind {\\n132: \\tcase waitFor:\\n133: \\t\\ttarget := redact.SafeString(\\\"holding lock\\\")\\n134: \\t\\tif !s.held {\\n135: \\t\\t\\ttarget = \\\"running request\\\"\\n136: \\t\\t}\\n137: \\t\\tw.Printf(\\\"wait for txn %s %s @ key %s (queuedLockingRequests: %d, queuedReaders: %d)\\\",\\n138: \\t\\t\\ts.txn.Short(), target, s.key, s.queuedLockingRequests, s.queuedReaders)\\n139: \\tcase waitSelf:\\n140: \\t\\tw.Printf(\\\"wait self @ key %s\\\", s.key)\\n141: \\tcase waitElsewhere:\\n142: \\t\\tif !s.held {\\n143: \\t\\t\\tw.SafeString(\\\"wait elsewhere by proceeding to evaluation\\\")\\n144: \\t\\t}\\n145: \\t\\tw.Printf(\\\"wait elsewhere for txn %s @ key %s\\\", s.txn.Short(), s.key)\\n146: \\tcase waitQueueMaxLengthExceeded:\\n147: \\t\\tw.Printf(\\\"wait-queue maximum length exceeded @ key %s with length %d\\\",\\n148: \\t\\t\\ts.key, s.queuedLockingRequests)\\n149: \\tcase doneWaiting:\\n150: \\t\\tw.SafeString(\\\"done waiting\\\")\\n151: \\tdefault:\\n152: \\t\\tpanic(\\\"unhandled waitingState.kind\\\")\\n153: \\t}\\n154: }\\n155: \\n156: // Implementation\\n157: // TODO(sbhola):\\n158: // - metrics about lockTable state to export to observability debug pages:\\n159: //   number of locks, number of waiting requests, wait time?, ...\\n160: \\n161: // The btree for keys that have locks on them.\\n162: type treeMu struct {\\n163: \\tmu syncutil.RWMutex // Protects everything in this struct.\\n164: \\n165: \\t// For assigning sequence numbers to the keyLocks objects as required by\\n166: \\t// the util/interval/generic type contract.\\n167: \\tlockIDSeqNum uint64\\n168: \\n169: \\t// Container for all keyLocks structs, inside which per-key lock tracking\\n170: \\t// is done. This includes information about the lock holder(s), requests being\\n171: \\t// sequenced through the lock table, and any queues that form on the key\\n172: \\t// because of read-write or write-write conflicts. Empty[1] keyLocks structs\\n173: \\t// are garbage collected. Additionally, keys which only have replicated locks\\n174: \\t// on them and no contending requests may also be garbage collected, as their\\n175: \\t// state can be recovered from persistent storage.\\n176: \\t//\\n177: \\t// [1] Keys that are not locked and have an empty wait-queue.\\n178: \\tbtree\\n179: \\n180: \\t// numKeysLocked tracks the number of keyLocks structs in the b-tree. It is\\n181: \\t// primarily used for constraining memory consumption. Ideally, we should be\\n182: \\t// doing better memory accounting than this.\\n183: \\tnumKeysLocked atomic.Int64\\n184: \\n185: \\t// For dampening the frequency with which we enforce\\n186: \\t// lockTableImpl.maxKeysLocked.\\n187: \\tlockAddMaxLocksCheckInterval uint64\\n188: }\\n189: \\n190: // lockTableImpl is an implementation of lockTable.\\n191: //\\n192: // Concurrency: in addition to holding latches, we require for a particular\\n193: // request ScanAndEnqueue() and CurState() must be called by the same\\n194: // thread.\\n195: //\\n196: // Mutex ordering: E.g. was it trying to perform a (possibly\n120: \t// locking) read or write an Intent?\n121: \tguardStrength lock.Strength\n122: }\n123: \n124: // String implements the fmt.Stringer interface.\n125: func (s waitingState) String() string {\n126: \treturn redact.StringWithoutMarkers(s)\n127: }\n128: \n129: // SafeFormat implements the redact.SafeFormatter interface.\n130: func (s waitingState) SafeFormat(w redact.SafePrinter, _ rune) {\n131: \tswitch s.kind {\n132: \tcase waitFor:\n133: \t\ttarget := redact.SafeString(\"holding lock\")\n134: \t\tif !s.held {\n135: \t\t\ttarget = \"running request\"\n136: \t\t}\n137: \t\tw.Printf(\"wait for txn %s %s @ key %s (queuedLockingRequests: %d, queuedReaders: %d)\",\n138: \t\t\ts.txn.Short(), target, s.key, s.queuedLockingRequests, s.queuedReaders)\n139: \tcase waitSelf:\n140: \t\tw.Printf(\"wait self @ key %s\", s.key)\n141: \tcase waitElsewhere:\n142: \t\tif !s.held {\n143: \t\t\tw.SafeString(\"wait elsewhere by proceeding to evaluation\")\n144: \t\t}\n145: \t\tw.Printf(\"wait elsewhere for txn %s @ key %s\", s.txn.Short(), s.key)\n146: \tcase waitQueueMaxLengthExceeded:\n147: \t\tw.Printf(\"wait-queue maximum length exceeded @ key %s with length %d\",\n148: \t\t\ts.key, s.queuedLockingRequests)\n149: \tcase doneWaiting:\n150: \t\tw.SafeString(\"done waiting\")\n151: \tdefault:\n152: \t\tpanic(\"unhandled waitingState.kind\")\n153: \t}\n154: }\n155: \n156: // Implementation\n157: // TODO(sbhola):\n158: // - This includes information about the lock holder(s), requests being\n171: \t// sequenced through the lock table, and any queues that form on the key\n172: \t// because of read-write or write-write conflicts. Empty[1] keyLocks structs\n173: \t// are garbage collected. Additionally, keys which only have replicated locks\n174: \t// on them and no contending requests may also be garbage collected, as their\n175: \t// state can be recovered from persistent storage.\n176: \t//\n177: \t// [1] Keys that are not locked and have an empty wait-queue.\n178: \tbtree\n179: \n180: \t// numKeysLocked tracks the number of keyLocks structs in the b-tree. It is\n181: \t// primarily used for constraining memory consumption. Ideally, we should be\n182: \t// doing better memory accounting than this.\n183: \tnumKeysLocked atomic.Int64\n184: \n185: \t// For dampening the frequency with which we enforce\n186: \t// lockTableImpl.maxKeysLocked.\n187: \tlockAddMaxLocksCheckInterval uint64\n188: }\n189: \n190: // lockTableImpl is an implementation of lockTable.\n191: //\n192: // Concurrency: in addition to holding latches, we require for a particular\n193: // request ScanAndEnqueue() and CurState() must be called by the same\n194: // thread.\n195: //\n196: // Mutex ordering:   lockTableImpl.enabledMu\n197: //\n198: //\t> treeMu.mu\n199: //\t> keyLocks.mu\n200: //\t> lockTableGuardImpl.mu\n201: ","url":"https://sourcegraph.sourcegraph.com/github.com/sg-evals/cockroach--v24.3.0@2e3aaf185d0ed9361efdbf04cd7cd1153477d912/-/blob/pkg/kv/kvserver/concurrency/lock_table.go%3FL1-200"}}}

The request must not have an existing guard.\\n84:  If a guard is returned, stores it for later use.\\n85: \\n86: acquire r=<name> k=<key> durability=r|u [ignored-seqs=<int>[-<int>][,<int>[-<int>]] strength=<strength>\\n87: ----\\n88: <error string>\\n89: \\n90:  Acquires lock for the request, using the existing guard for that request.\\n91: \\n92: release txn=<name> span=<start>[,<end>]\\n93: ----\\n94: <error string>\\n95: \\n96:  Releases locks for the named transaction.\\n97: \\n98: update txn=<name> ts=<int>[,<int>] epoch=<int> span=<start>[,<end>] [ignored-seqs=<int>[-<int>][,<int>[-<int>]]]\\n99: ----\\n100: <error string>\\n101: \\n102:  Updates locks for the named transaction.\\n103: \\n104: pushed-txn-updated txn=<name> status=committed|aborted|pending [ts=<ts>]\\n105: ----\\n106: \\n107:  Informs the lock table that the named transaction is finalized.\\n108: \\n109: add-discovered r=<name> k=<key> txn=<name> [lease-seq=<seq>] [consult-txn-status-cache=<bool>] [strength=<strength>]\\n110: ----\\n111: <error string>\\n112: \\n113:  Adds a discovered lock that is discovered by the named request.\\n114: \\n115: check-opt-no-conflicts r=<name> spans=none|shared|update|exclusive|intent@<start>[,<end>]+...\\n116: ----\\n117: no-conflicts: <bool>\\n118: \\n119:  Checks whether the request, which previously called ScanOptimistic, has no lock conflicts.\\n120: \\n121: is-key-locked-by-conflicting-txn r=<name> k=<key> strength=<strength>\\n122: ----\\n123: locked: <bool>\\n124: \\n125:  Checks whether the provided key is locked by a conflicting transaction.\\n126: \\n127: dequeue r=<name>\\n128: ----\\n129: <error string>\\n130: \\n131:  Calls lockTable.Dequeue for the named request. The request and guard are\\n132:  discarded after this.\\n133: \\n134: guard-state r=<name>\\n135: ----\\n136: new|old: state=<state> [txn=<name> ts=<ts>]\\n137: \\n138:   Calls lockTableGuard.NewStateChan in a non-blocking manner, followed by\\n139:   CurState.\\n140: \\n141: should-wait r=<name>\\n142: ----\\n143: <bool>\\n144: \\n145:  Calls lockTableGuard.ShouldWait.\\n146: \\n147: resolve-before-scanning r=<name>\\n148: ----\\n149: <intents to resolve>\\n150: \\n151: enable [lease-seq=<seq>]\\n152: ----\\n153: \\n154:  Calls lockTable.Enable.\\n155: \\n156: clear [disable]\\n157: ----\\n158: <state of lock table>\\n159: \\n160:  Calls lockTable.Clear. Optionally disables the lockTable.\\n161: \\n162: print\\n163: ----\\n164: <state of lock table>\\n165: \\n166: query span=<start>[,<end> | /Max] [max-locks=<int>] [max-bytes=<int>] [uncontended]\\n167: ----\\n168: \\n169:  Queries the lockTable over a given span (or over the entire LT if no span\\n170:  provided), returning lock state info up to a maximum number of locks or bytes\\n171:  if provided.  By default only returns contended locks (those with waiters),\\n172:  unless the uncontended option is given.\\n173: \\n174: \\n175: metrics\\n176: ----\\n177: <metrics for lock table>\\n178: \\n179: // in order to detect dependency cycles between transactions or coordinator\\n37: // failure of conflicting transactions.\\n38: var LockTableDeadlockOrLivenessDetectionPushDelay = settings.RegisterDurationSetting(\\n39: \\tsettings.SystemOnly,\\n40: \\t\\\"kv.lock_table.deadlock_detection_push_delay\\\",\\n41: \\t\\\"the delay before pushing in order to detect dependency cycles between transactions\\\",\\n42: \\t// Transactions that come across contending locks must push the lock holder\\n43: \\t// to detect whether they're abandoned or are part of a dependency cycle that\\n44: \\t// would cause a deadlock. We optimistically assume failed transaction\\n45: \\t// coordinators and deadlocks are not common in production applications, and\\n46: \\t// wait locally on locks for a while before checking for a deadlock. This\\n47: \\t// reduces needless pushing in cases where there is no deadlock or coordinator\\n48: \\t// failure to speak of.\\n49: \\t//\\n50: \\t// Assuming we care about detecting failed coordinators more than deadlocks\\n51: \\t// (which are a true rarity and a case we don't want to optimize for),\\n52: \\t// increasing the value of this setting would come down to optimizing the cost\\n53: \\t// we pay per-abandoned transaction. Currently, with the txnStatusCache,\\n54: \\t// we pay a cost per abandoned transaction per range. The txnStatusCache is\\n55: \\t// also quite small. Some improvements here could be:\\n56: \\t// - A per-store cache instead of a per-range cache.\\n57: \\t// - Increasing the number of finalized transactions we keep track of, even if\\n58: \\t// this is per-request instead of per-range.\\n59: \\t//\\n60: \\t// The deadlock portion of this setting is analogous to Postgres'\\n61: \\t// deadlock_timeout setting, which has a default value of 1s:\\n62: \\t//  https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/runtime-config-locks.html#GUC-DEADLOCK-TIMEOUT.\\n63: \\t//\\n64: \\t// When increasing this value, we should be conscious that even once\\n65: \\t// distributed deadlock detection begins, there is some latency proportional\\n66: \\t// to the length of the dependency cycle before the deadlock is detected.\\n67: \\t//\\n68: \\t//\\n69: \\t// TODO(nvanbenschoten): increasing this default value.\\n70: \\t100*time.Millisecond,\\n71: \\tsettings.WithName(\\\"kv.lock_table.deadlock_detection_or_liveness_push_delay\\\"),\\n72: \\t// Old setting name when liveness and deadlock push delays could be set\\n73: \\t// independently.\\n74: \\tsettings.WithRetiredName(\\\"kv.lock_table.coordinator_liveness_push_delay\\\"),\\n75: )\\n76: \\n77: // lockTableWaiterImpl is an implementation of lockTableWaiter.\\n78: type lockTableWaiterImpl struct {\\n79: \\tnodeDesc *roachpb.NodeDescriptor\\n80: \\tst       *cluster.Settings\\n81: \\tclock    *hlc.Clock\\n82: \\tstopper  *stop.Stopper\\n83: \\tir       IntentResolver\\n84: \\tlt       lockTable\\n85: \\n86: \\t// When set, WriteIntentErrors are propagated instead of pushing\\n87: \\t// conflicting transactions.\\n88: \\tdisableTxnPushing bool\\n89: \\t// When set, called just before each push timer event is processed.\\n90: \\tonPushTimer func()\\n91: }\\n92: \\n93: // IntentResolver is an interface used by lockTableWaiterImpl to push\\n94: // transactions and to resolve intents. Copyright 2020 The Cockroach Authors.\n//\n// Use of this software is governed by the CockroachDB Software License\n// included in the /LICENSE file.\n\npackage concurrency\n\nimport (\n\t\"context\"\n\t\"testing\"\n\t\"time\"\n\n\t\"github.com/cockroachdb/cockroach/pkg/kv/kvpb\"\n\t\"github.com/cockroachdb/cockroach/pkg/kv/kvserver/concurrency/isolation\"\n\t\"github.com/cockroachdb/cockroach/pkg/kv/kvserver/concurrency/lock\"\n\t\"github.com/cockroachdb/cockroach/pkg/kv/kvserver/lockspanset\"\n\t\"github.com/cockroachdb/cockroach/pkg/kv/kvserver/spanset\"\n\t\"github.com/cockroachdb/cockroach/pkg/kv/kvserver/txnwait\"\n\t\"github.com/cockroachdb/cockroach/pkg/roachpb\"\n\tclustersettings TestWriteWriteConflictDetection verifies that the lock table correctly detects\n// write-write conflicts between two transactions.\nfunc TestWriteWriteConflictDetection(t *testing.T) {\n\tdefer leaktest.AfterTest(t)()\n\tdefer log.Scope(t).Close(t)\n\n\tctx := context.Background()\n\tsettings := clustersettings.MakeTestingClusterSettings()\n\tclock := hlc.NewClockForTesting(nil)\n\n\t// Create a lock table with capacity for this test\n\tlt := newLockTable(10000, roachpb.RangeID(1), clock, settings)\n\tlt.enabled = true\n\tlt.enabledSeq = 1\n\n\t// Create two transactions with different UUIDs\n\ttxn1 := &enginepb.TxnMeta{\n\t\tID:             uuid.Must(uuid.NewV4()),\n\t\tIsoLevel:       isolation.Serializable,\n\t\tEpoch:          1,\n\t\tWriteTimestamp: hlc.Timestamp{WallTime: 1000},\n\t\tMinTimestamp:   hlc.Timestamp{WallTime: 1000},\n\t\tPriority:       roachpb.NormalUserPriority,\n\t}\n\ttxn2 := &enginepb.TxnMeta{\n\t\tID:             uuid.Must(uuid.NewV4()),\n\t\tIsoLevel:       isolation.Serializable,\n\t\tEpoch:          1,\n\t\tWriteTimestamp: hlc.Timestamp{WallTime: 1000},\n\t\tMinTimestamp:   hlc.Timestamp{WallTime: 1000},\n\t\tPriority:       roachpb.NormalUserPriority,\n\t}\n\n\t// Create requests for the same key with intent locks\n\tkey := roachpb.Key(\"key1\")\n\n\t// First request: txn1 writes to key1\n\treq1 := Request{\n\t\tTxn:       txn1,\n\t\tTimestamp: txn1.WriteTimestamp,\n\t\tLockSpans: lockspanset.LockSpanSet{},\n\t\tLatchSpans: spanset.SpanSet{},\n\t}\n\treq1.LockSpans.AddLocks(lock.Intent, span(key, key.Next()))\n\n\t// Second request: txn2 writes to the same key1\n\treq2 := Request{\n\t\tTxn:       txn2,\n\t\tTimestamp: txn2.WriteTimestamp,\n\t\tLockSpans: lockspanset.LockSpanSet{},\n\t\tLatchSpans: spanset.SpanSet{},\n\t}\n\treq2.LockSpans.AddLocks(lock.Intent, span(key, key.Next()))\n\n\t// Scan and enqueue first request\n\tguard1, err := lt.ScanAndEnqueue(&req1, nil)\n\trequire.NoError(t, err)\n\trequire.NotNil(t, guard1)\n\trequire.False(t, guard1.ShouldWait(), \"first request should not wait\")\n\n\t// Acquire lock for first request\n\terr = lt.AcquireLock(txn1, lock.Intent, guard1)\n\trequire.NoError(t, err)\n\n\t// Scan and enqueue second request - should find conflict\n\tguard2, err := lt.ScanAndEnqueue(&req2, nil)\n\trequire.NoError(t, err)\n\trequire.NotNil(t, guard2)\n\trequire.True(t, guard2.ShouldWait(), \"second request should wait for first transaction's lock\")\n\n\t// Check the waiting state\n\tstate, err := guard2.CurState()\n\trequire.NoError(t, err)\n\trequire.Equal(t, waitFor, state.kind)\n\trequire.Equal(t, txn1.ID, state.txn.ID)\n\trequire.True(t, state.held)\n\n\t// Release the locks\n\tlt.Dequeue(guard1)\n\tlt.Dequeue(guard2)\n}\n\n// TestReadWriteConflictWithSerializableIsolation verifies that serializable\n// transactions detect read-write conflicts.\nfunc TestReadWriteConflictWithSerializableIsolation(t *testing.T) {\n\tdefer leaktest.AfterTest(t)()\n\tdefer log.Scope(t).Close(t)\n\n\tctx := context.Background()\n\tsettings := clustersettings.MakeTestingClusterSettings()\n\tclock := hlc.NewClockForTesting(nil)\n\n\tlt := newLockTable(10000, roachpb.RangeID(1), clock, settings)\n\tlt.enabled = true\n\tlt.enabledSeq = 1\n\n\tkey := roachpb.Key(\"key1\")\n\n\ttxn1 := &enginepb.TxnMeta{\n\t\tID:             uuid.Must(uuid.NewV4()),\n\t\tIsoLevel:       isolation.Serializable,\n\t\tEpoch:          1,\n\t\tWriteTimestamp: hlc.Timestamp{WallTime: 1000},\n\t\tMinTimestamp:   hlc.Timestamp{WallTime: 1000},\n\t\tPriority:       roachpb.NormalUserPriority,\n\t}\n\ttxn2 := &enginepb.TxnMeta{\n\t\tID:             uuid.Must(uuid.NewV4()),\n\t\tIsoLevel:       isolation.Serializable,\n\t\tEpoch:          1,\n\t\tWriteTimestamp: hlc.Timestamp{WallTime: 1001},\n\t\tMinTimestamp:   hlc.Timestamp{WallTime: 1001},\n\t\tPriority:       roachpb.NormalUserPriority,\n\t}\n\n\t// First transaction writes to key\n\treq1 := Request{\n\t\tTxn:       txn1,\n\t\tTimestamp: txn1.WriteTimestamp,\n\t\tLockSpans: lockspanset.LockSpanSet{},\n\t\tLatchSpans: spanset.SpanSet{},\n\t}\n\treq1.LockSpans.AddLocks(lock.Intent, span(key, key.Next()))\n\n\tguard1, err := lt.ScanAndEnqueue(&req1, nil)\n\trequire.NoError(t, err)\n\trequire.False(t, guard1.ShouldWait())\n\n\terr = lt.AcquireLock(txn1, lock.Intent, guard1)\n\trequire.NoError(t, err)\n\n\t// Second transaction tries to read the same key\n\t// In serializable isolation, reads should also detect conflicts with writes\n\treq2 := Request{\n\t\tTxn:       txn2,\n\t\tTimestamp: txn2.WriteTimestamp,\n\t\tLockSpans: lockspanset.LockSpanSet{},\n\t\tLatchSpans: spanset.SpanSet{},\n\t}\n\treq2.LockSpans.AddLocks(lock.Shared, span(key, key.Next()))\n\n\tguard2, err := lt.ScanAndEnqueue(&req2, nil)\n\trequire.NoError(t, err)\n\trequire.NotNil(t, guard2)\n\trequire.True(t, guard2.ShouldWait(), \"read should wait for write lock\")\n\n\tlt.Dequeue(guard1)\n\tlt.Dequeue(guard2)\n}\n\n// TestTransactionPriorityConflictResolution verifies that transaction priority\n// influences conflict resolution.\nfunc TestTransactionPriorityConflictResolution(t *testing.T) {\n\tdefer leaktest.AfterTest(t)()\n\tdefer log.Scope(t).Close(t)\n\n\tsettings := clustersettings.MakeTestingClusterSettings()\n\tclock := hlc.NewClockForTesting(nil)\n\n\tlt := newLockTable(10000, roachpb.RangeID(1), clock, settings)\n\tlt.enabled = true\n\tlt.enabledSeq = 1\n\n\tkey := roachpb.Key(\"key1\")\n\n\t// Low priority transaction writes first\n\ttxn1 := &enginepb.TxnMeta{\n\t\tID:             uuid.Must(uuid.NewV4()),\n\t\tIsoLevel:       isolation.Serializable,\n\t\tEpoch:          1,\n\t\tWriteTimestamp: hlc.Timestamp{WallTime: 1000},\n\t\tMinTimestamp:   hlc.Timestamp{WallTime: 1000},\n\t\tPriority:       roachpb.LowUserPriority,\n\t}\n\n\t// High priority transaction tries to write to the same key\n\ttxn2 := &enginepb.TxnMeta{\n\t\tID:             uuid.Must(uuid.NewV4()),\n\t\tIsoLevel:       isolation.Serializable,\n\t\tEpoch:          1,\n\t\tWriteTimestamp: hlc.Timestamp{WallTime: 1001},\n\t\tMinTimestamp:   hlc.Timestamp{WallTime: 1001},\n\t\tPriority:       roachpb.HighUserPriority,\n\t}\n\n\t// First transaction (low priority) acquires lock\n\treq1 := Request{\n\t\tTxn:       txn1,\n\t\tTimestamp: txn1.WriteTimestamp,\n\t\tLockSpans: lockspanset.LockSpanSet{},\n\t\tLatchSpans: spanset.SpanSet{},\n\t}\n\treq1.LockSpans.AddLocks(lock.Intent, span(key, key.Next()))\n\n\tguard1, err := lt.ScanAndEnqueue(&req1, nil)\n\trequire.NoError(t, err)\n\terr = lt.AcquireLock(txn1, lock.Intent, guard1)\n\trequire.NoError(t, err)\n\n\t// Second transaction (high priority) tries to write\n\treq2 := Request{\n\t\tTxn:       txn2,\n\t\tTimestamp: txn2.WriteTimestamp,\n\t\tLockSpans: lockspanset.LockSpanSet{},\n\t\tLatchSpans: spanset.SpanSet{},\n\t}\n\treq2.LockSpans.AddLocks(lock.Intent, span(key, key.Next()))\n\n\tguard2, err := lt.ScanAndEnqueue(&req2, nil)\n\trequire.NoError(t, err)\n\n\t// High priority transaction should wait, but could potentially push low priority\n\trequire.True(t, guard2.ShouldWait())\n\tstate, err := guard2.CurState()\n\trequire.NoError(t, err)\n\trequire.Equal(t, txn1.ID, state.txn.ID)\n\n\tlt.Dequeue(guard1)\n\tlt.Dequeue(guard2)\n}\n\n// TestLockAcquisitionTimout verifies that lock acquisition can timeout.\nfunc TestLockAcquisitionTimeout(t *testing.T) {\n\tdefer leaktest.AfterTest(t)()\n\tdefer log.Scope(t).Close(t)\n\n\tsettings := clustersettings.MakeTestingClusterSettings()\n\tclock := hlc.NewClockForTesting(nil)\n\n\tlt := newLockTable(10000, roachpb.RangeID(1), clock, settings)\n\tlt.enabled = true\n\tlt.enabledSeq = 1\n\n\tkey := roachpb.Key(\"key1\")\n\n\ttxn1 := &enginepb.TxnMeta{\n\t\tID:             uuid.Must(uuid.NewV4()),\n\t\tIsoLevel:       isolation.Serializable,\n\t\tEpoch:          1,\n\t\tWriteTimestamp: hlc.Timestamp{WallTime: 1000},\n\t\tMinTimestamp:   hlc.Timestamp{WallTime: 1000},\n\t\tPriority:       roachpb.NormalUserPriority,\n\t}\n\ttxn2 := &enginepb.TxnMeta{\n\t\tID:             uuid.Must(uuid.NewV4()),\n\t\tIsoLevel:       isolation.Serializable,\n\t\tEpoch:          1,\n\t\tWriteTimestamp: hlc.Timestamp{WallTime: 1001},\n\t\tMinTimestamp:   hlc.Timestamp{WallTime: 1001},\n\t\tPriority:       roachpb.NormalUserPriority,\n\t}\n\n\t// First transaction acquires lock\n\treq1 := Request{\n\t\tTxn:       txn1,\n\t\tTimestamp: txn1.WriteTimestamp,\n\t\tLockSpans: lockspanset.LockSpanSet{},\n\t\tLatchSpans: spanset.SpanSet{},\n\t}\n\treq1.LockSpans.AddLocks(lock.Intent, span(key, key.Next()))\n\n\tguard1, err := lt.ScanAndEnqueue(&req1, nil)\n\trequire.NoError(t, err)\n\terr = lt.AcquireLock(txn1, lock.Intent, guard1)\n\trequire.NoError(t, err)\n\n\t// Second transaction tries to acquire with a timeout\n\treq2 := Request{\n\t\tTxn:            txn2,\n\t\tTimestamp:      txn2.WriteTimestamp,\n\t\tLockSpans:      lockspanset.LockSpanSet{},\n\t\tLatchSpans:     spanset.SpanSet{},\n\t\tLockWaitPolicy: lock.WaitPolicy_Block,\n\t}\n\treq2.LockSpans.AddLocks(lock.Intent, span(key, key.Next()))\n\n\tguard2, err := lt.ScanAndEnqueue(&req2, nil)\n\trequire.NoError(t, err)\n\trequire.True(t, guard2.ShouldWait())\n\n\t// In a real scenario with a deadline, the wait would eventually timeout\n\t// For this test, we just verify the wait state is set up correctly\n\tstate, err := guard2.CurState()\n\trequire.NoError(t, err)\n\trequire.Equal(t, waitFor, state.kind)\n\n\tlt.Dequeue(guard1)\n\tlt.Dequeue(guard2)\n}\n\n// TestIntentResolutionAfterCommit verifies that locks are released after\n// transaction commit.\nfunc TestIntentResolutionAfterCommit(t *testing.T) {\n\tdefer leaktest.AfterTest(t)()\n\tdefer log.Scope(t).Close(t)\n\n\tsettings := clustersettings.MakeTestingClusterSettings()\n\tclock := hlc.NewClockForTesting(nil)\n\n\tlt := newLockTable(10000, roachpb.RangeID(1), clock, settings)\n\tlt.enabled = true\n\tlt.enabledSeq = 1\n\n\tkey := roachpb.Key(\"key1\")\n\n\ttxn1 := &enginepb.TxnMeta{\n\t\tID:             uuid.Must(uuid.NewV4()),\n\t\tIsoLevel:       isolation.Serializable,\n\t\tEpoch:          1,\n\t\tWriteTimestamp: hlc.Timestamp{WallTime: 1000},\n\t\tMinTimestamp:   hlc.Timestamp{WallTime: 1000},\n\t\tPriority:       roachpb.NormalUserPriority,\n\t}\n\ttxn2 := &enginepb.TxnMeta{\n\t\tID:             uuid.Must(uuid.NewV4()),\n\t\tIsoLevel:       isolation.Serializable,\n\t\tEpoch:          1,\n\t\tWriteTimestamp: hlc.Timestamp{WallTime: 1001},\n\t\tMinTimestamp:   hlc.Timestamp{WallTime: 1001},\n\t\tPriority:       roachpb.NormalUserPriority,\n\t}\n\n\t// First transaction acquires lock\n\treq1 := Request{\n\t\tTxn:       txn1,\n\t\tTimestamp: txn1.WriteTimestamp,\n\t\tLockSpans: lockspanset.LockSpanSet{},\n\t\tLatchSpans: spanset.SpanSet{},\n\t}\n\treq1.LockSpans.AddLocks(lock.Intent, span(key, key.Next()))\n\n\tguard1, err := lt.ScanAndEnqueue(&req1, nil)\n\trequire.NoError(t, err)\n\terr = lt.AcquireLock(txn1, lock.Intent, guard1)\n\trequire.NoError(t, err)\n\n\t// Second transaction tries to access the same key\n\treq2 := Request{\n\t\tTxn:       txn2,\n\t\tTimestamp: txn2.WriteTimestamp,\n\t\tLockSpans: lockspanset.LockSpanSet{},\n\t\tLatchSpans: spanset.SpanSet{},\n\t}\n\treq2.LockSpans.AddLocks(lock.Intent, span(key, key.Next()))\n\n\tguard2, err := lt.ScanAndEnqueue(&req2, nil)\n\trequire.NoError(t, err)\n\trequire.True(t, guard2.ShouldWait(), \"should wait for first transaction's lock\")\n\n\t// Release the lock from first transaction (simulating commit)\n\tlt.Dequeue(guard1)\n\n\t// After dequeue, second transaction's wait state should be updated\n\t// Re-scan to check the new state\n\tguard2_rescan, err := lt.ScanAndEnqueue(&req2, guard2)\n\trequire.NoError(t, err)\n\tif guard2_rescan != nil && guard2_rescan.ShouldWait() {\n\t\t// Still waiting on something else or lock not fully resolved\n\t\tlt.Dequeue(guard2_rescan)\n\t} else {\n\t\tlt.Dequeue(guard2)\n\t}\n}\n\n// TestIntentResolutionAfterAbort verifies that locks are released after\n// transaction abort.\nfunc uuid.Must(uuid.NewV4()),\n\t\tIsoLevel:       isolation.Serializable,\n\t\tEpoch:          1,\n\t\tWriteTimestamp: hlc.Timestamp{WallTime: 1000},\n\t\tMinTimestamp:   hlc.Timestamp{WallTime: 1000},\n\t\tPriority:       roachpb.NormalUserPriority,\n\t}\n\n\t// Create requests for the same key with intent locks\n\tkey := roachpb.Key(\"key1\")\n\n\t// First request: txn1 writes to   1,\n\t\tWriteTimestamp: hlc.Timestamp{WallTime: 1001},\n\t\tMinTimestamp:   hlc.Timestamp{WallTime: 1001},\n\t\tPriority:       roachpb.NormalUserPriority,\n\t}\n\n\t// First transaction acquires TestIntentResolutionAfterCommit(t *testing.T) {\n\tdefer leaktest.AfterTest(t)()\n\tdefer log.Scope(t).Close(t)\n\n\tsettings := clustersettings.MakeTestingClusterSettings()\n\tclock := When disabled,\\n208: \\t// no locks or wait-queues are maintained.\\n209: \\t//\\n210: \\t// enabledMu is held in read-mode when determining whether the lockTable\\n211: \\t// is enabled and when acting on that information (e.g. adding new locks).\\n212: \\t// It is held in write-mode when enabling or disabling the lockTable.\\n213: \\t//\\n214: \\t// enabledSeq holds the lease sequence for which the lockTable is enabled\\n215: \\t// under. This\\n222: \\t// is to preserve fairness despite the design choice of allowing\\n223: \\t// out-of-order evaluation of requests with overlapping spans where the\\n224: \\t// latter request does not encounter contention. This out-of-order\\n225: \\t// evaluation happens because requests do not reserve spans that are\\n226: \\t// uncontended while they wait for on contended locks after releasing their\\n227: \\t// latches. Consider the following examples:\\n228: \\t//\\n229: \\t// Example 1:\\n230: \\t// - req1 wants to write to A, B\\n231: \\t// - req2 wants to write to B\\n232: \\t// - lock at A is held by some other txn.\\n233: \\t// - Even though req2 arrives later, req1 will wait only in the queue for A\\n234: \\t//   and allow req2 to proceed to evaluation.\\n235: \\t//\\n236: \\t// Example 2:\\n237: \\t// - Same as example 1 but lock at A is held by txn3 and lock at B is held\\n238: \\t//   by txn4.\\n239: \\t// - Lock at A is released so req1 claims the lock at A and starts waiting at\\n240: \\t//   B.\\n241: \\t// - It is unfair for req1 to wait behind req2 at B. The sequence number\\n242: \\t//   assigned to req1 and req2 will restore the fairness by making req1\\n243: \\t//   wait before req2.\\n244: \\t//\\n245: \\t// Example 3: Deadlock in lock table if it did not use sequence numbers.\\n246: \\t// - Lock at B is acquired by txn0.\\n247: \\t// - req1 (from txn1) arrives at lockTable and wants to write to A and B.\\n248: \\t//   It queues at B.\\n249: \\t// - req2 (from txn2) arrives at lockTable and only wants to write A.\\n250: \\t//   It proceeds to evaluation and acquires the lock at A for txn2 and then\\n251: \\t//   the request is done. After a call to ScanAndEnqueue() (which is made while holding\\n324: // latches), the caller must first call lockTableGuard.StartWaiting() and if it\\n325: // returns true release the latches and continue interacting with the\\n326: // lockTableGuard. If StartWaiting() returns false, the request can proceed to\\n327: // evaluation.\\n328: //\\n329: // Waiting logic: The interface hides the queues that the request is waiting on,\\n330: // and the request's position in the queue. One of the reasons for this hiding\\n331: // is that queues are not FIFO since a request that did not wait on a queue for\\n332: // key k in a preceding call to ScanAndEnqueue() (because k was not locked and\\n333: // there was no queue) may need to wait on the queue in a later call to\\n334: // ScanAndEnqueue(). So sequencing of requests arriving at the lockTable is\\n335: // partially decided by a sequence number assigned to a request when it first\\n336: // called ScanAndEnqueue() and queues are ordered by this sequence number.\\n337: // However the sequencing is not fully described by the sequence numbers -- a\\n338: // request R1 encountering contention over some keys in its span does not\\n339: // prevent a request R2 that has a higher sequence number and overlapping span\\n340: // to proceed if R2 does not encounter contention. This concurrency (that is not\\n341: // completely fair) is deemed desirable.\\n342: //\\n343: // The interface exposes an abstracted version of the waiting logic in a way\\n344: // that the request that starts waiting is considered waiting for at most one\\n345: // other request or transaction. This is exposed as a series of state\\n346: // transitions where the transitions are notified via newState() and the current\\n347: // state can be read using CurState().\\n348: //\\n349: //   - The waitFor* states provide information on who the request is waiting\\n350: //     for.\\n351: //\\n352: //   - The waitElsewhere state is a rare state that is used when the lockTable is\\n353: //     under memory pressure and is clearing its internal queue state. Like the\\n354: //     waitFor* states, it informs the request who it is waiting for so that\\n355: //     deadlock detection works. However, sequencing information inside the\\n356: //     lockTable is mostly discarded.\\n357: //\\n358: //   - The waitSelf state is a rare state when a different request from the same\\n359: //     transaction has claimed the lock. See the comment about the concept of\\n360: //     claiming a lock on claimantTxn().\\n361: //\\n362: //   - The waitQueueMaxLengthExceeded state is used to indicate that the request\\n363: //     was rejected because it attempted to enter a lock wait-queue as a locking\\n364: //     request and found that the queue's length was already equal to or\\n365: //     exceeding the request's configured maximum.\\n366: //\\n367: //   - The doneWaiting state is used to indicate that the request should make\\n368: //     another call to ScanAndEnqueue() (that next call is more likely to return a\\n369: //     lockTableGuard that returns false from StartWaiting()).\\n370: type lockTableGuardImpl struct {\\n371: \\tseqNum uint64\\n372: \\tlt     *lockTableImpl\\n373: \\n374: \\t// Information about this request.\\n375: \\ttxn                *roachpb.Transaction\\n376: \\tts                 hlc.Timestamp\\n377: \\tspans              *lockspanset.LockSpanSet\\n378: \\twaitPolicy         lock.WaitPolicy\\n379: \\tmaxWaitQueueLength int\\n380: \\n381: \\t// Snapshot of the tree for which this request has some spans. When enabled, the lockTable tracks locks and\n207: \t// allows requests to queue in wait-queues on these locks. When disabled,\n208: \t// no locks or wait-queues are maintained.\n209: \t//\n210: \t// enabledMu is held in read-mode when determining whether the lockTable\n211: \t// is enabled and when acting on that information (e.g. adding new locks).\n212: \t// It is held in write-mode when enabling or disabling the lockTable.\n213: \t//\n214: \t// enabledSeq holds the lease sequence for which the lockTable is enabled\n215: \t// under. Discovered locks from prior lease sequences are ignored, as they\n216: \t// may no longer be accurate.\n217: \tenabled    bool\n218: \tenabledMu  syncutil.RWMutex\n219: \tenabledSeq roachpb.LeaseSequence\n220: \n221: \t// A sequence number is assigned to each request seen by the lockTable. This\n222: \t// is to preserve fairness despite the design choice of allowing\n223: \t// out-of-order evaluation of requests with overlapping spans where the\n224: \t// latter request does not encounter contention. This out-of-order\n225: \t// evaluation happens because requests do not reserve spans that are\n226: \t// uncontended while they wait for on contended locks after releasing their\n227: \t// latches. Consider the following examples:\n228: \t//\n229: \t// Example 1:\n230: \t// - req1 wants to write to A, B\n231: \t// - req2 wants to write to B\n232: \t// - lock at A is held by some other txn.\n233: \t// - Even though req2 arrives later, req1 will wait only in the queue for A\n234: \t//   and allow req2 to proceed to evaluation.\n235: \t//\n236: \t// Example 2:\n237: \t// - Same as example 1 but lock at A is held by txn3 and lock at B is held\n238: \t//   by txn4.\n239: \t// - Lock at A is released so req1 claims the lock at A and starts waiting at\n240: \t//   B.\n241: \t// - It is unfair for req1 to wait behind req2 at B. The sequence number\n242: \t//   assigned to req1 and req2 will restore the fairness by making req1\n243: \t//   wait before req2.\n244: \t//\n245: \t// Example 3: Deadlock in lock table if it did not use sequence numbers.\n246: \t// - Lock at B is acquired by txn0.\n247: \t// - req1 (from txn1) arrives at lockTable and wants to write to A and B.\n248: \t//   It queues at B.\n249: \t// - req2 (from txn2) arrives at lockTable and only wants to write A.\n250: \t//   It proceeds to evaluation and acquires the lock at A for txn2 and then\n251: \t//   the request is done. The lock is still held.\n252: \t// - req3 (from txn3) wants to write to A and B. It queues at A.\n253: \t// - txn2 releases A. req3 is in the front of the queue at A so it claims the\n254: \t//   lock and starts waiting at B behind req1.\n255: \t// - txn0 releases B. req1 gets to claim the lock at B and does another scan\n256: \t//   and adds itself to the queue at A, behind req3 which holds the claim for\n257: \t//   A.\n258: \t// Now in the queues for A and B req1 is behind req3 and vice versa and\n259: \t// this deadlock has been created entirely due to the lock table's behavior.\n260: \tseqNum atomic.Uint64\n261: \n262: \t// locks contains the btree object (wrapped in the treeMu structure) that\n263: \t// contains the actual keyLocks objects. These keyLocks objects represent the\n264: \t// individual keys (that may have one or more locks on them) in the lock\n265: \t// table. Locks on both Global and Local keys are stored in the same btree.\n266: \tlocks treeMu\n267: \n268: \t// maxKeysLocked is a soft maximum on amount of per-key lock information\n269: \t// tracking[1]. When it is exceeded, and subject to the dampening in\n270: \t// lockAddMaxLocksCheckInterval, locks will be cleared.\n271: \t//\n272: \t// [1] Simply put, the number of keyLocks objects in the lockTable btree.\n273: \tmaxKeysLocked int64\n274: \t// When maxKeysLocked is exceeded, will attempt to clear down to minKeysLocked,\n275: \t// instead of clearing everything.\n276: \tminKeysLocked int64\n277: \n278: \t// txnStatusCache is a small LRU cache that tracks the status of\n279: \t// transactions that have been successfully pushed.\n280: \t//\n281: \t// NOTE: it probably makes sense to maintain a single txnStatusCache\n282: \t// across all Ranges on a Store instead of an individual cache per\n283: \t// Range. After a call to ScanAndEnqueue() (which is made while holding\n324: // latches), the caller must first call lockTableGuard.StartWaiting() and if it\n325: // returns true release the latches and continue interacting with the\n326: // lockTableGuard. If StartWaiting() returns false, the request can proceed to\n327: // evaluation.\n328: //\n329: // Waiting logic: The interface hides the queues that the request is waiting on,\n330: // and the request's position in the queue. One of the reasons for this hiding\n331: // is that queues are not FIFO since a request that did not wait on a queue for\n332: // key k in a preceding call to ScanAndEnqueue() (because k was not locked and\n333: // there was no queue) may need to wait on the queue in a later call to\n334: // ScanAndEnqueue(). So sequencing of requests arriving at the lockTable is\n335: // partially decided by a sequence number assigned to a request when it first\n336: // called ScanAndEnqueue() and queues are ordered by this sequence number.\n337: // However the sequencing is not fully described by the sequence numbers -- a\n338: // request R1 encountering contention over some keys in its span does not\n339: // prevent a request R2 that has a higher sequence number and overlapping span\n340: // to proceed if R2 does not encounter contention. This concurrency (that is not\n341: // completely fair) is deemed desirable.\n342: //\n343: // The interface exposes an abstracted version of the waiting logic in a way\n344: // that the request that starts waiting is considered waiting for at most one\n345: // other request or transaction. This is exposed as a series of state\n346: // transitions where the transitions are notified via newState() and the current\n347: // state can be read using CurState().\n348: //\n349: //   - The waitFor* states provide information on who the request is waiting\n350: //     for.\n351: //\n352: //   - The waitElsewhere state is a rare state that is used when the lockTable is\n353: //     under memory pressure and is clearing its internal queue state. Like the\n354: //     waitFor* states, it informs the request who it is waiting for so that\n355: //     deadlock detection works. However, sequencing information inside the\n356: //     lockTable is mostly discarded.\n357: //\n358: //   - The waitSelf state is a rare state when a different request from the same\n359: //     transaction has claimed the lock. See the comment about the concept of\n360: //     claiming a lock on claimantTxn().\n361: //\n362: //   - The waitQueueMaxLengthExceeded state is used to indicate that the request\n363: //     was rejected because it attempted to enter a lock wait-queue as a locking\n364: //     request and found that the queue's length was already equal to or\n365: //     exceeding the request's configured maximum.\n366: //\n367: //   - The doneWaiting state is used to indicate that the request should make\n368: //     another call to ScanAndEnqueue() (that next call is more likely to return a\n369: //     lockTableGuard that returns false from StartWaiting()).\n370: type lockTableGuardImpl struct {\n371: \tseqNum uint64\n372: \tlt     *lockTableImpl\n373: \n374: \t// Information about this request.\n375: \ttxn                *roachpb.Transaction\n376: \tts                 hlc.Timestamp\n377: \tspans              *lockspanset.LockSpanSet\n378: \twaitPolicy         lock.WaitPolicy\n379: \tmaxWaitQueueLength int\n380: \n381: \t// Snapshot of the tree for which this request has some spans. Note that\n382: \t// the lockStates in this snapshot may have been removed from\n383: \t// lockTableImpl. Additionally, it is possible that there is a new keyLocks\n384: \t// struct for the same key. This can result in various harmless anomalies:\n385: \t// - the request may hold a claim on a keyLocks struct that is no longer\n386: \t//   in the tree. When it next does a scan, it will either find a new\n387: \t//   keyLocks struct where it will compete or none.
